# Technical Risk Register

**Last updated:** 2026-08-14 (C-293/C-294/C-295 — severity that is not connected to tracked work, a god-class allowlist with no size ratchet, and dependency checking that runs in only one direction; C-221 tier raised 4→3; C-216 locations re-measured)
**Governing ADR:** ADR-044 (Technical Risk Register)
**Entry count:** 295 concerns (180 resolved) + 41 disagreements — 5 relocated to views-reporting

> **Release gate — pipeline-core 3.1.1 — OPEN, prepared 2026-08-14.**
> A patch release. It exists because the first CRAF'd delivery could not run: an orphaned
> call killed it, and the command the error told the operator to run could neither reach
> their collection nor repair it (#473). views-postprocessing installs from PyPI, so the
> fix cannot reach them without a release.
>
> Disposition:
>
> - ~~**C-290**~~ **closed** — the orphaned `self._create_attribute_by_type` call, and the
>   two callers whose unchecked failure path it was masking. Both guarded before the call
>   was deleted, because deleting it alone would have turned a loud crash into a silent
>   duplicate upload.
> - ~~**C-291**~~ **closed** — `ensure-collection` reporting OK while writing nothing, and
>   being unable to target a partner collection at all.
> - **C-292 — CONSCIOUSLY ACCEPTED.** The provisioning CLI creates collections open to
>   `Role.any()` while creating locked-down buckets. Undocumented, undecided, and now more
>   reachable because this release lets the CLI create partner collections. Not changed
>   here: tightening permissions on partner-facing metadata has an external blast radius
>   and a hotfix unblocking a delivery is the wrong place to decide it.
> - **C-233 — status corrected, not reopened.** It was recorded Resolved by #331 while a
>   create-on-read branch survived in the same method. Now genuinely resolved and pinned
>   against a real `AppwriteException`.
> - **C-193**, **C-206**, **C-287**, **C-288**, **C-289** — inherited unchanged.
>
> **Found after 3.1.1 was published, dispositioned here because the guard's date window
> puts them in this cycle.** All three come from an audit of our own governance artifacts,
> not from the release: they are about how we track work, not about anything shipped in
> 3.1.1. None affects the published wheel — recorded so the next gate inherits a decision
> rather than an open question.
>
> - **C-293 — CONSCIOUSLY CARRIED.** Severity is not connected to tracked work: 11 of 28
>   open Tier 1/2 concerns cite no issue at all, and the sole open Tier 1 cites only closed
>   ones. Fixing this means deciding how the register and the issue tracker relate, which is
>   a governance decision and not a patch-release one.
> - **C-294 — CONSCIOUSLY CARRIED.** The god-class allowlist has no size ratchet; +236 LOC
>   of growth inside exempted classes is invisible to a guard that passes 3/3. The fix is
>   cheap (freeze the LOC alongside the name) but it will fail on four classes the day it
>   lands, and that is a piece of work, not a hotfix.
> - **C-295 — CONSCIOUSLY CARRIED.** Dependency checking runs declared→installed only, so
>   13 undeclared runtime imports are invisible. Adding the reverse direction requires first
>   deciding which of the 13 are deliberate — see C-216.
>
> **Verified before the gate was written:** the full suite passes with the two new guards
> mutation-checked in both directions — reinstating the orphaned call fails
> `test_no_orphaned_self_calls.py` naming the class and line; disabling the caller guards
> fails `test_hash_lookup_failure_is_not_absence.py` with the exact CRAF'd log line.

> **Release gate — pipeline-core 3.1.0 — ~~OPEN~~ SHIPPED 2026-08-14.**
> `views-pipeline-core 3.1.0` is on PyPI (tag `3.1.0`, commit `b9f8fb1`). Verified from
> outside rather than from this worktree — a fresh venv, `pip install
> views-pipeline-core==3.1.0` from PyPI: **all 61 shipped modules import**, `pytest` is
> absent from the consumer install, all four additions views-impact waited for are
> reachable, both `evaluation_sequencing` schemes are present, and the no-suffix artifact
> name is unchanged from 3.0.x. That procedure is C-206's standing remedy and this
> release inherited C-206 as accepted, so it was run rather than assumed.
> Prepared 2026-08-13; the gate below was its disposition.
> A **minor** release: epic #458 adds public API rather than fixing a defect. Four new
> public things — `targets_suffix` on two callables, `ModelPathManager.get_processed_data_file_paths`,
> `WandBModule.log_yearly_evaluation` — plus the `evaluation_sequencing` config key
> (ADR-060). Backwards compatible; `tests/test_public_surface_requires_a_major_bump.py`
> passes and does not force anything, which is the guard behaving correctly rather than
> permission to skip the reasoning.
>
> Disposition, in the gate's own wording (*"closed or consciously accepted"*):
>
> - **C-288 — CONSCIOUSLY ACCEPTED.** views-impact is a live consumer with no owner and no
>   tests, and its conformance check sits in this repo because it has nowhere else to live.
>   Accepted rather than fixed: giving another repository a test suite is not something
>   this one can do, and the alternative — removing our stopgap — would leave the boundary
>   unchecked entirely. Recorded so the next release inherits a decision.
> - **C-289 — CONSCIOUSLY ACCEPTED.** The consumer half of the boundary check runs only
>   where sibling checkouts exist, so it never runs in CI. A standing limitation, not a
>   defect: no runner can see another repository. Same acceptance as the wire-fixture drift
>   check and the SessionAuth ratchet.
> - **C-193 / #374**, **C-206 / #274** — inherited unchanged from 3.0.0 and 3.0.1. This
>   release changes none of the 52 tracked public names, so C-193 is not being leaned on.
> - **C-287** — dispositioned at 3.0.1 and unchanged. The sniffer still checks that a
>   metric key is present and not that its contents are valid.
>
> **Verified before the gate was written**, by a falsification audit of the readiness
> claim: the wheel contains every new module and ships zero tests; a clean venv installs it
> and imports all 61 modules; the full suite passes in a CI-shaped workspace with no
> siblings (2226 passed, 38 skipped — fifteen more than locally, exactly the sibling checks
> standing down); and every new test file passes run alone. The audit's two findings are
> C-288 and C-289 above, both about the record rather than the code.

> **Release gate — pipeline-core 3.0.1 — ~~OPEN~~ SHIPPED 2026-08-11.**
> `views-pipeline-core 3.0.1` is on PyPI (tag `3.0.1`, commit `b108d96`). Verified from
> outside rather than from this worktree — a fresh venv, `pip install
> views-pipeline-core==3.0.1` from PyPI: all **61** shipped modules import, `pytest` is
> absent from the consumer install, `UpdateViewser` resolves through the lazy re-export,
> and `combined_targets` pools the gate channel. That procedure is C-206's standing
> remedy and this release inherited C-206 as accepted, so it was run, not assumed.
> Prepared 2026-08-11; the gate below was its disposition.
> A patch release carrying the gate-pooling fix (**C-286**) and the three named-debt
> refactors. Disposition, in the gate's own wording (*"closed or consciously accepted"*):
>
> - ~~**C-286**~~ **closed** (#422, #432). The defect this release exists for. Both
>   `_build_context` sites route through `combined_targets`; both managers' tests cover it.
> - ~~**C-164**~~ **narrowed** (#431) — the dumping-ground half is fixed; the
>   `_detect_data_source` OCP half is untouched and still open. Splitting the file did not
>   make the loader open to extension, and closing the entry would have retired the one
>   tracking the real defect.
> - **C-193 / #374 — still consciously accepted**, unchanged from 3.0.0. Nothing enforces
>   that a breaking public-symbol change forces a major bump beyond the 52-name snapshot.
>   This release is a patch and changes none of those 52, so the acceptance is not being
>   leaned on here — but it is inherited, not resolved.
> - **C-206 / #274 — still consciously accepted**, unchanged. Verify from a clean venv
>   after publishing, not from this worktree. The 3.0.0 gate's own clean-room procedure
>   applies verbatim.
> - **C-287 — CONSCIOUSLY ACCEPTED.** Found *during* this release cycle, by views-models
>   reviewing guidance this repo sent them (views-models#372). `CoreConfigSniffer` checks a
>   metric key is *present* and never that its contents are *valid*, so it certifies configs
>   `NativeEvaluator._validate_config` refuses. Accepted rather than fixed: teaching the
>   sniffer to validate names is a **new failure mode at config load**, and adding one to a
>   patch release that is already on `main` and drafted is how a patch stops being a patch.
>   The failure it permits is loud and downstream, not silent. Pinned as executable current
>   behaviour, and the metric-key constants are now conformance-checked against
>   `NativeEvaluator._METRIC_LIST_KEYS` so the drift that produced it cannot recur silently.
> - **views-faoapi payload conformance — OPEN by dependency**, not by choice. The only
>   honest fixture has to come from that repo; requested as views-faoapi#380. The Appwrite
>   seam pin is checked; the payload shape is not.
>
> **The version choice is itself a gate item.** 3.0.1 rather than 4.0.0 was argued both
> ways and decided on evidence: no live downstream config carries the retired `targets`
> key, and if one did the failure is a `ValueError` naming the key and its replacement.
> The #188 incident the major-bump guard exists for was a *silent* break. Reasoning is in
> `CHANGELOG.md` under 3.0.1 so a future contributor inherits a decision rather than
> rediscovering an open question.

> **Release gate — pipeline-core 3.0.0 — ~~CLOSED~~ SHIPPED 2026-08-03.**
> `views-pipeline-core 3.0.0` is on PyPI. Verified from outside rather than from the

> `pip install views-reporting` and `pip install views-baseline` — both unresolvable

>
> Gate disposition, in the gate's own wording (*"closed or consciously accepted"*):
> ~~**C-227**, **C-228**, **C-231**~~ closed (#329/#330/#331) · ~~**C-236**~~ closed (both
> shelves audited; FAO shelf wiped with views-faoapi's approval) · ~~**C-190**~~ closed as
> *will not pin* (#375) · ~~views-evaluation bump~~ done (`^1.0.0`) ·
> **C-193 / #374 — CONSCIOUSLY ACCEPTED**: nothing enforces that a breaking public-symbol
> change forces a major bump, and this release *was* that event. The bump was reasoned by
> hand and both engines were migrated first. The guard is tracked in #374 and is not
> scheduled. · **C-206 / #274 — CONSCIOUSLY ACCEPTED**: proofs run against editable
> worktrees. It does not affect the published artifact — CI builds from a clean install,
> and the clean-room verification above used PyPI, not a local tree. The check is now
> derived rather than hardcoded (#391) and reports real problems in five repos.
>
> **Both acceptances are decisions, not gaps.** Recording them is the point: the next
> release inherits a decision rather than rediscovering an open question.

> **Strategic context (2026-06-19) — `views-frames` is coming.** A new leaf
> data-contract package, `views_platform/views-frames` (scaffolded 2026-06-19,
> README only so far), is the **planned landing** for the data-handling /
> god-class refactor. It will own the array-transport contract behind published
> Protocols (`PredictionFrame`/`FeatureFrame` + `SpatioTemporalIndex`), numpy-only,
> at the root of the dependency DAG. Several concerns below are tagged with how it
> mitigates them — most importantly C-36, C-135, C-182, C-165, C-40/C-66, C-167.
> **No refactor is underway; this is preparedness so the work is not started blind.**

---

## Format

| Field | Description |
|-------|-------------|
| **ID** | `C-xx` for concerns, `D-xx` for disagreements |
| **Tier** | 1 (critical) — 4 (minor) |
| **Description** | What the risk is |
| **Trigger** | When this risk becomes actionable |
| **Source** | Where this risk was identified |
| **Status** | Open / Mitigated / Accepted |

### Tier Definitions

| Tier | Severity | Response |
|------|----------|----------|
| 1 | Critical — correctness or data integrity at risk | Must be addressed before next release |
| 2 | High — architectural degradation or silent failure | Should be addressed within current development cycle |
| 3 | Medium — maintainability or operational risk | Track and address opportunistically |
| 4 | Low — minor or cosmetic concern | Document and defer |

---

## Causal Clusters

*Added by review-rr strategic (2026-06-19). The 77 open concerns reduce to ~8 root
causes. **Clusters A and B share one keystone — `views-frames` — making it the
single highest-leverage investment in the register** (Cluster A alone holds 5 of
the 10 Tier-2 concerns).*

| Cluster | Root cause | Member concerns | Keystone fix |
|---------|-----------|-----------------|--------------|
| **A — Data-Contract Gap** | No canonical typed array contract; DataFrames + the `_ViewsDataset` god class are the de-facto internal transport | C-36 *(keystone)*, C-135, C-182, C-40, C-66, C-48, C-165, C-164, C-167, C-67, C-176, C-141, C-38, **C-186 *(first observed in prod)***, C-187, C-191, C-190/C-193 *(both narrowed to residuals)* | **views-frames — LANDED.** Re-scoped 2026-07-31: the keystone shipped (PF is leaf-owned; reconciliation runs through an injected `Reconciler` port, #217). The adoption-execution cohort is closed — C-188 (superseded by the port), C-189 (golden oracle written), C-192 (merged → C-206) — and C-190/C-193 survive only as residuals, both **3.0.0 release-gate items**. **Live residual: C-36, C-135, C-40/C-66, C-164, C-165** |
| **B — Cross-repo coupling / cycle** | pipeline-core ↔ views-reporting circular dependency; no cross-repo contract test | C-30, C-47, C-50, C-144, C-167, C-168, C-59, C-62; D-27, D-28 *(C-105/106/113/114/133 relocated → views-reporting C-26/C-22/C-107)* | **views-frames** (neutral leaf) + cross-repo contract tests |
| **C — God classes (SRP/ISP)** | Oversized classes | C-01, C-35, C-36, C-44, C-45 | Strangler extraction |
| **D — Ensemble inheritance (LSP)** | EnsembleManager inherits ForecastingModelManager | C-55, C-65, C-03, C-67, C-141, C-93, C-158; D-03/13/17 | Composition (DataFrameEnsembleManager) |
| **E — Fail-Loud gaps** | Silent failures vs the project's own "Fail Loud" principle | C-26, C-61, C-166, C-170, C-178, C-176 | Narrow catches / raise |
| **F — output_scale governance** | Scale-awareness contract incomplete | C-174, C-175; D-29/30/31 | ADR-055 enforcement |
| **G — Test coverage** | Unit-heavy; no integration / cross-repo tests | C-10, C-16, C-21, C-30, C-58, C-108 | Integration + contract tests |
| **H — External-service fragility** | viewser / wandb / pandas-lock *(Appwrite members ceded to Cluster I, 2026-07-31)* | C-57, C-101, C-112, C-173, C-179 | Timeouts / version policy |
| **J — "No" is not the same as "I don't know"** *(added 2026-08-01)* | **A system that cannot distinguish an absent answer from an unobtainable one, and answers anyway.** Twenty instances over nine months, eleven already fixed **individually** — the class was never registered, so every new surface reintroduced it. Five of the seven all-time Tier-1 entries belong to it, and on 2026-08-01 it recurred **twice inside the tool built to detect it**, producing 436 phantom orphan-file reports against production and four rounds of false diagnosis including advice to delete production buckets. The three sub-shapes: a **failed** read read as absence (C-231, C-71/C-77, C-80, C-170), a **partial** read read as completeness (C-241, C-242, C-26), and a **discarded** result read as success (C-227, C-232, C-249). | **Open:** C-26, C-61, C-166, C-170, C-178, C-145, C-152, C-256, **C-257 (found BY the guard on its first run — the mechanism works)**. **Resolved 2026-08-01 by the S0–S3 sweep:** C-256, C-258 *(T1)*. **By the S0–S4 sweep:** C-259 (the guard's own blind spot). **Resolved 2026-08-01 by epic #339 (#341, #342) — the two Tier-1s and the four that fed them:** C-241 *(T1)*, C-249 *(T1)*, C-242, C-243, C-244, C-250, C-251. **Resolved earlier, retained as evidence the class is real:** C-54 *(T1)*, C-68, C-71, C-77, C-80, C-72, C-49, C-73, C-56, C-227 *(T1)*, C-231 *(T1)*, C-232 *(T1)*. **Epic #339 close-out (2026-08-01): three Tier-1s closed (C-241, C-249, C-258), the keystone guard shipped (#343) and was itself found scope-wrong twice (C-259, C-261) before it held. Both of the cluster's live Tier-1s at the epic's start are now closed**; what remains open is the four the keystone guard admits it cannot reach (C-26, C-61, and the C-183/184/185 blind spots) plus the enumeration work in C-256 | **A rule plus one executable guard** — *a read that can be partial or can fail must return that fact with its content, and no caller may use the content without disposing of that fact* — enforced by an AST test in the idiom of `test_boundary_enforcement.py` / `test_import_purity.py`, banning (i) a `list_*` call with no `Query.limit`, (ii) a bare `except Exception` in the storage modules, (iii) a discarded `OperationResult`. **Honest limits: it does not reach C-61 (staleness is not incompleteness), C-26 (truncation inside an adapter, not at a read), C-249 (the read was marked incomplete; the renderer ignored it), or C-183/184/185 (you cannot guard what you never observe)** — four of twenty. This is the register's first cluster whose keystone is a **rule**, not a package. |
| **I — Appwrite seam adopted before its contract** *(added 2026-07-30, þing-01; re-scoped 2026-07-31)* | **The surface was built to make a demo work and never re-contracted.** Auto-provisioning, production defaults, in-band failure returns, ambient `.env` sourcing and one fanned-out key are not five defects — they are one decision ("optimise for *it just works on my machine*"), which ADR-046 §5 later *ratified* rather than questioned. The belief-mirroring tests and the god-class shape are the same cause: nothing ever forced the surface to be described from outside. **Not an artifact of one day's work** — the members were found by five independent audits across four months (Apr: C-11/C-15; Jun: C-166/C-177; Jul 27: C-217–C-221; Jul 30: C-227–C-230). Predictive value: expect the next defect wherever this surface still auto-heals. | **C-227, C-228, C-231, C-232, C-233, C-236 — all Resolved/Mitigated 2026-07-31 (#329/#330/#331/#332)**; still open: **C-253 *(the seam is a hard dependency of the platform's keystone package)***, ~~C-241~~, ~~C-249~~ (**both Tier 1, resolved 2026-08-01 by #341/#342**), ~~C-242~~, ~~C-243~~, ~~C-244~~, C-245, C-229, C-230, C-234, C-235, C-237, C-238, C-177, C-11, C-166, C-15, C-217, C-218, C-220, C-221, C-44 (C-250/C-251 also resolved by #342) | **PLATFORM-001 + `coordinate_registry.toml`** (views-appwrite, ratified 2026-07-28) executed here via #322–#325 |

**Cluster A sequencing (the keystone track):** views-frames protocols → migrate the
~56 cross-repo `_ViewsDataset` sites (C-135) → decompose `_ViewsDataset` (C-36) →
de-mutate reconciliation (C-182) → typed reconciliation boundary (C-167) → relocate
ReconciliationModule (D-28). **C-36 is gated on views-frames existing; doing it first
is the trap C-36's DANGER note prevents.**

## Open Concerns

| ID | Tier | Description | Trigger | Source | Status |
|----|------|-------------|---------|--------|--------|
| C-203 | 3 | **Raw-cache readers bypass the dataloader grid-name normalization seam.** `_normalize_grid_index` (`dataloaders.py`) normalizes `priogrid_gid`→`priogrid_id` only on the `get_data` fetch + cache-read path. `evaluation/stage.py:161` (`_load_actuals`) and `reporting/stage.py:156/281/304` call `read_dataframe` on the raw cache directly, so in a transitional state where an old on-disk cache still carries `priogrid_gid` (not re-fetched), actuals/historical carry `gid` while predictions carry the canonical `id`. Masked in-repo (`pd.Index.equals` ignores names); the real exposure is cross-repo name-sensitive alignment (views-evaluation/views-reporting adapters). Self-closes as caches are re-fetched; the durable fix routes those reads through the loader or normalizes `read_dataframe`. Introduced by consolidation PR-2; revisit in PR-3 or a follow-up. | When an old `priogrid_gid` cache is evaluated/reported without re-fetching and feeds name-sensitive cross-repo alignment | review (PR-2 priogrid_id consolidation) | Open |
| C-204 | 3 | **priogrid_id consolidation incomplete — three dormant `gid→id` renames remain (PR-3 deferred).** After the PR-2 seam (#258, `adb05da`) normalizes the grid name at ingest, the renames in `data/handlers.py:86` (`_PGDataset`), `managers/ensemble/ensemble.py:30` and `managers/ensemble/dataframe_ensemble.py:47` (`_ENTITY_RENAME`) are no-ops, and the sniffer + aggregator still accept both names. PR-3 (delete them + flip sniffer id-only) is **deliberately deferred**: the `_PGDataset` rename is load-bearing as a safety net — the aggregator (`aggregation/aggregator.py:797`) defensively accepts `gid`, then builds `PGMDataset`, whose `validate_indices` requires `priogrid_id`; the line-86 rename bridges that just in time. Deleting it while the aggregator admits `gid`, or while old `gid` caches still exist on disk, turns a tolerated path into a hard failure. Tracked: GitHub #259. Related: C-203. | When PR-3 is attempted — delete the renames ONLY together with tightening `aggregator.py:797` to id-only AND after old `gid` caches have aged out (no `gid` reaches `_PGDataset`) | review (PR-2 consolidation, deferral decision) | Open |
| C-206 | 2 | **Publish-gate proofs can silently run against non-development editable worktrees.** All platform envs install sibling repos editable, so a proof run executes whatever branch each working tree happens to be on. At audit time views-hydranet sat on `feat/zinb-distributional-head` (62 ahead / 2 behind development, 6 dirty tracked files) — an S1 run would have "proven" experimental engine code and validated the wrong thing, with green output indistinguishable from a valid proof. Generalizes C-192's "dev box masks it" (editable state ≠ released/target state) from #181 to every publish-gate proof. No mechanism pins worktree state before a proof. Live check: `tests/test_falsification_s1_readiness.py::test_s1_hydranet_worktree_on_development` (RUN_S1_READINESS=1). Mitigation options: pre-run branch assertion in the proof procedure, or a dedicated `git worktree` checkout of development for proof runs. **ABSORBED C-192 (2026-07-31, review-rr strategic)** — the #181-specific instance: `views_reporting` is installed editable off `feat/adopt-views-frames`, so a local report run exercises the *fixed* dense consumer rather than production's object-dtype path; `tests/test_managers/test_report_stage_memory.py` characterises pipeline-core's converter overhead only, not the released views-reporting consumer that actually OOMs. **Second live instance (2026-07-31): the dev box has `views-frames 1.8.0` installed against a `^1.8` pin while the release train assumes 1.10.0** — 3.0.0 will be validated against a different leaf than it ships onto. Related: C-192 (Resolved — merged here), C-190 (declared-floor residual, same class), C-207. | When any publish-gate proof (Epic #261 S1, the #181 close-out validation, or the 3.0.0 release validation) is launched without first asserting every editable run-path repo — and every leaf version — matches the intended target state | falsify (2026-07-05, S1 readiness) + review-rr strategic (2026-07-31, C-192 merge) | Open |
| C-207 | 2 | **S1 run-scale vs disk capacity: originally sized at ~300 GB against 26 GB free (volume 98% full) — the sizing did not survive contact with the config.** rusty_bucket at declared scale (8 constituents × `n_samples=128` × 471,960 pgm rows × 6 targets × 13 origins) writes ≈151 GB of constituent eval `y_pred.npy` + ≈151 GB pooled ensemble output (S=1024). The box volume has 26 GB free; the run dies mid-way after GPU-hours (or fills the disk to 100%, destabilizing everything else on the box) — the fetch/train/eval pipeline has no disk-budget preflight anywhere. Complements C-40/C-66 (RAM-scale) with the storage dimension of the same sample-scale cluster. Resolution is a conscious scale choice (fewer constituents / lower `n_samples` for the proof — which weakens the C-66 memory-proof aspect — or freed/added storage). Live check: `tests/test_falsification_s1_readiness.py::test_s1_disk_headroom_for_rusty_bucket_at_declared_samples` (RUN_S1_READINESS=1). Related: C-40, C-66. | When S1 (or any multi-constituent PFE evaluation at n_samples ≥ 64) is launched without a disk-budget preflight against the target volume | falsify (2026-07-05, S1 readiness) | **Resolved (2026-08-04) — the requirement was never 300 GB after 2026-07-20, and nobody noticed.** The estimate was arithmetically correct when made (2026-07-05) and its assumptions then changed twice: the ensemble was thinned from 128 samples per constituent to **16** on 2026-07-20 — for a MEMORY reason, stated in `config_hyperparameters.py`'s own comment — and `rusty_bucket` declares **3** regression targets, not the 6 assumed. Derived from the config as it stands: 8 constituents x 16 samples x 471,960 rows (13,110 land cells x 36 steps) x 3 targets x 13 origins x 4B, plus a pooled S=128 output = **~19 GB**, not ~300. Confirmed by reproduction: feeding the July assumptions back into the new derivation returns 150.8 GB, matching the original '≈151 GB' exactly — so the estimate was never wrong, the config moved and the number did not follow. **The gate test no longer hardcodes a total.** `test_s1_disk_headroom_for_rusty_bucket_at_declared_samples` now reads `expected_models`, `expected_samples_per_model` and `regression_targets` from the ensemble config and requires 2x the derived output; if the config cannot be read it SKIPS rather than asserting a guessed figure. Proven live: changing any of the three inputs moves the estimate. This is the seventh time a hand-written figure in a guard has gone stale here (C-259, C-261, C-264, #346, C-277, the editable-tree check) — the fix is the same one every time, derive it. **Disk is no longer an S1 blocker**: the operator reclaimed 106 superseded prediction runs on 2026-08-04, taking free space from 29 GB to 183 GB. |
| C-211 | 4 | **The pandas parquet cache writes non-atomically — the frame cache now has stage-then-swap integrity, its older sibling does not.** `save_dataframe`/the `get_data` cache writes (`files/utils.py:206`, `dataloaders.py` cache path) call `to_parquet(path)` directly; a crash mid-write leaves a truncated parquet at the live cache name. The narrowed `except (ConnectionError, TimeoutError, OSError, AppwriteException)` never fires on these paths because nothing raises. Net: a delivery that did not happen produces a success log and a green run. Tier 1: forecast artifacts are silently absent from (or orphaned in) the store with an actively misleading operator signal — the failure has no error channel at all. **Scope qualifier (review-rr 2026-07-31): this is silent *delivery* loss, not data loss.** Predictions are persisted locally first (NpzSaver / LocalParquetSaver, the three-destination design in ADR-047), so the artifacts still exist on the run box; what is lost is their arrival at the store the external consumer reads — which is precisely what the FAO contract depends on. PLATFORM-001 §6 ("a half-succeeded write raises") governs; fix scheduled in #322. Guarded by `test_prediction_savers.py::TestAppwriteSaver::test_in_band_failure_is_reported_not_swallowed` and `test_prediction_io.py::TestAppwriteInBandFailure`. | C-230 | 3 | **Credential exposure surface across carriers is unaudited, and `AppwriteConfig`'s generated `__repr__` renders the API key.** `AppwriteConfig` is a plain `@dataclass` (`file.py:217`) whose first field is `credentials: Union[str, Dict[str, str]]` with no `field(repr=False)` — so `repr(config)`, an f-string log of the config, a W&B run-config capture, or any traceback rendering locals prints the live key. Verified today: no such call exists at the two module sites (`file.py`, `datastore.py` greps clean), so the exposure is **latent — one f-string away**, which is why it is Tier 3 and not higher. PLATFORM-001 §5's redaction clause is multi-carrier and binding (env var, config field, request header, netrc, keychain); no audit of logs/W&B configs against it is on record for this repo. Fix scheduled in #325 (one-pass audit + `repr=False` on the secret field). Related: C-229 (same dataclass), C-11. | A developer adds a config-level debug log, passes the Appwrite config into a W&B run config, or an unhandled exception renders locals from the upload path | þing-01 orð_08 §2 / Á-14 → #325 grounding (2026-07-30, dataclass verified) | **Resolved (2026-07-31, #325)** — `credentials` on `AppwriteConfig` and `api_key` on `PredictionStoreConfig` (the same live key, second instance found during the audit) are now `field(repr=False)`, so no repr, f-string, `str()` or traceback renders them; endpoints and coordinates still render, since a repr that hid everything would push people back to printing the raw object (PLATFORM-001 §5: credentials never, endpoints may). Pinned by `tests/test_modules/test_credential_redaction.py` for both configs and for session-auth email/password. **Audit result (the deliverable, recorded because a clean audit is a finding):** three sweeps over `views_pipeline_core/` — (i) no log or print renders a config *object*; every hit was `self.configs['name']`, a single model-config key, or a docstring example; (ii) no `logger.*` call takes a credential-named value; (iii) W&B receives `config=self.configs`, the model config from `config_meta.py`, and `managers/configuration/` reads no environment variable at all — so no credential can reach a run config by that route. **Scope of the fix, stated precisely rather than as "closed":** `repr=False` suppresses `__repr__`, so f-strings, `str()`, `print()` and traceback rendering can no longer expose the key — the accidental path this concern described. It does **not** cover `dataclasses.asdict()`, `astuple()`, `vars()` or `__dict__`, all of which still return the value (verified by execution, and characterised in the test file so the limit is visible rather than assumed). That residual matters on one path in particular: `modules/wandb/utils.py` uses `asdict(entry)` at three sites and `vars(item)` at two — the W&B route this entry named as a hazard — though those helpers take `EvaluationMetrics` instances, not configs, so no config reaches them today. **And the audit's own limits:** the three sweeps were regex over `logger.*` call shapes and would miss `logger.info("%s", cfg)`, a differently-named variable, or a credential assigned to an intermediate first. The honest claim is *no evidence of a leak across three sweep shapes*, not *no leak exists* — the same standard this repo applied to þing-02's history scans |
| C-231 | 1 | **A failed bucket read is treated as proof of absence, and absence triggers a DESTRUCTIVE metadata delete.** `upload_file_with_metadata` de-duplicates by (a) finding the metadata document by hash, then (b) verifying the file exists in storage via `self.get_file(bucket_id, existing_file_id)` (`file.py:2118-2121`). If (b) is not successful it concludes the document is a leftover orphan and **deletes the production metadata document** (`file.py:2131-2140`), with the delete's own failure only `logger.warning`-ed. But `get_file` (`file.py:2718-2727`) catches **every** `AppwriteException` and collapses it into one `success=False` — so *file genuinely absent*, *wrong/renamed bucket id*, *key lacks read scope on that bucket*, and *any other API error* are indistinguishable at the call site. A read failure therefore destroys a valid index card for a file that does exist, and consumers search by metadata, so the forecast becomes **invisible** while remaining on disk in the store. Composes with the cluster: wrong coordinate → card deleted → C-228 provisions a phantom bucket → duplicate uploaded there → C-227 discards the result and logs `"Forecasts uploaded to Appwrite Datastore successfully."` **Evidential status (stated plainly): the mechanism is verified by reading both functions; that a live run reaches the branch is NOT yet proven.** Network-level faults likely propagate as non-`AppwriteException` types and would not trigger it; bucket-not-found and permission-denied are ordinary API errors and almost certainly would. Tier 1 despite the unproven trigger: the operation is destructive, silent, and acts on the only record consumers can search by — the same standard under which C-198 was registered before any incident. **Sequencing hazard — this is why it is urgent now: PLATFORM-001 §5's three-tier keys (read / write-object / provision, #322) are a machine for producing permission-denied on exactly that bucket read.** A key correctly scoped for its tier could begin deleting valid metadata the day it is issued. | C-241 | 1 | **`search_files_by_metadata` returns one page and calls it the answer; `get_latest_file_id` then returns the newest of the OLDEST 25 matches.** `AppwriteMetadataHandler.search_files_by_metadata` (`modules/appwrite/file.py`) calls `self.databases.list_documents(db_id, coll_id, queries=queries)` with **no `Query.limit`**, so Appwrite applies its default page size of 25. The helper receives the true `total` in the response and returns `{"documents": …, "total": …}` — handing the caller the evidence of its own incompleteness, which no caller compares. `DatastoreModule.get_predictions_by_metadata` then sorts those ≤25 rows client-side by `$createdAt` descending and `get_latest_file_id` takes `[0]`. Appwrite's default order is creation-ascending, so the page is the *oldest* 25 and the "latest" is stale — correct until the 26th matching document exists, then silently wrong and staler every run. **Blast radius, precisely:** views-postprocessing's `_ContractStorePort.latest_file_id` feeds ADR-013 `resolve_run`, whose **manifest** lookup uses the broad `HOP_A_MANIFEST_FILTERS = {"category": "forecast", "type": "sampled_forecast_manifest"}` — every manifest ever published matches, so the run selected can be an old one. Its **shard** lookups add `"name": entry["name"]`, which is unique and matches ≤ a few rows, so they resolve fine *for that old run* — the delivery therefore **assembles and ships a stale run as current rather than failing**. **CORRECTED 2026-08-01 (expert-code-review): views-faoapi is NOT affected, and it holds the reference fix.** faoapi does not import pipeline-core; it keeps its own copy of this helper at `views-faoapi:src/views_faoapi/managers/appwrite/metadata.py:412`, and that copy **pages correctly** — it hit this defect, diagnosed it, and closed it under its own **#287** with a comment naming the mechanism verbatim: *"Appwrite's `list_documents` caps a page at its default (25) unless a `Query.limit` is supplied — and a single `limit(100)` is still short of a 108-shard wire run… a silently-truncated result drops shards and refuses an otherwise-complete run (25/108 → '83 missing' → ingest_failed). Sibling methods (`list_files`, `list_buckets`) already paginate this way; this one was missed."* So `manager.py:129` calls a FIXED helper. **Narrowed by falsification 2026-08-01 — the earlier blanket "faoapi is NOT affected" was itself over-broad:** faoapi fixed exactly ONE method under #287 (`metadata.py:453`); three other `list_documents` calls in the same file (`:348`, `:373`, `:495`) appear unpaged and are **unverified**. The narrow claim survives — the path this entry names reaches a paged helper — but faoapi's other metadata paths are not covered by that, and belong in a views-faoapi issue rather than an assertion here. The affected set is therefore **pipeline-core, and views-postprocessing by inheritance** (it imports `DatastoreModule`) — which also means one fix here repairs both, at the cost of a version bump and no work in the consumer. Port faoapi's implementation rather than re-deriving it. See C-252 for why nobody looked. Tier 1: wrong output, no error signal, on the path that serves an external party. **Reachability unverified** — it depends on the manifest count exceeding 25, which is one read-only query away and must be run before this entry is actioned or downgraded. **RESOLVED 2026-08-01 (#341, S1 of epic #339).** `search_files_by_metadata` now walks with `Query.limit(DEFAULT_PAGE_LIMIT)`/`Query.offset`, terminating on an EMPTY page rather than a short one and advancing the offset by what it RECEIVED rather than what it asked for — a server that caps a page below the request no longer truncates the walk. `APPWRITE_DEFAULT_PAGE_SIZE = 25` is now a named constant so no walk in the module inherits the default, and `MAX_METADATA_PAGES` bounds a substrate that ignores `offset`. The walk is certified against the server's own `total`: a mismatch returns `SEARCH_INCOMPLETE` rather than a short list. **A second, unregistered half was found during the fix and is repaired in the same change:** `DatastoreModule.get_predictions_by_metadata` (`datastore.py:440`) and `list_all_predictions_unfiltered` (`:732`) both converted a failed search into `[]`, which `get_latest_file_id` turned into `None` — so making the search fail loudly would merely have swapped a false-stale answer for a false-absent one. Both now raise `MetadataSearchIncomplete`. Cross-repo consequence, verified: views-postprocessing's `resolve_run` already raises `SourceSelectionError` on `None`, with the message *"nothing has been published under the contract"* — true for a real absence, false for a broken read. It now receives an honest exception instead; no silent regression, and the diagnosis improves. Covered by `tests/test_modules/test_appwrite_pagination.py` (8 tests against a double built from the SDK's own query encoding, which returns 25 rows when no limit is supplied) and `test_datastore.py::TestSearchFailureIsNotAbsence` (4). The pre-existing `test_appwrite.py::test_search_files_by_metadata` had to be repaired, not weakened: its `Mock.return_value` returned the same page for every offset — a substrate that ignores `offset`, which the new walk correctly refuses to certify. That is C-218 in miniature. | The 26th document matching any broad filter — in practice, the next FAO delivery once >25 manifests exist | **Known trade, accepted:** `reported_total` is captured from the first page, so a document inserted or deleted *during* the walk also yields `SEARCH_INCOMPLETE` — a race becomes a loud, retryable delivery failure, and no caller retries today. Accepted deliberately: a rare loud failure is the outcome this entry exists to obtain, and a count nobody verified must not be delivered. Revisit if the FAO path ever reports it in practice. | expert-code-review (2026-08-01, post-mortem of the reconcile.py false alarm) | **Resolved** |

A short file read would therefore produce phantom *dangling documents* — the identical defect mirrored, in the same function pair, after the lesson. The two walks live in `reconcile/walk.py` under one stated rule — *a listing that was truncated, failed, or disagrees with the substrate's own count is recorded in `indeterminate`, never returned as if it were whole* — and the symmetry is asserted by `TestC242FileWalkIsCertifiedToo` rather than hoped for. They are deliberately still two functions: they page different APIs with different response shapes and failure signals, and the unifying abstraction is not yet known (WET until a third caller shows its shape). **Second finding, caught by review-diff in the same changeset:** both walks initially kept the short-page terminator (`len(batch) < PAGE_SIZE`) and a fixed `page * PAGE_SIZE` stride — the exact substrate behaviour #341 had fixed in `file.py` hours earlier, rewritten here one story later. Against a server that grants 40 rows when asked for 100 the walk collected 40 of 500; the total-guard caught it, so nothing was silently wrong, but the audit reported INDETERMINATE about a bucket it could have read perfectly. Both walks now terminate on an EMPTY page and advance by what they received. **This is C-242's own finding — "the same defect, mirrored, after the lesson" — recurring at story scale rather than function scale**, which is the more interesting observation: the fix was one story old and still not generalised by its author. | expert-code-review (2026-08-01, correctness audit of the fix) + /code-review max (2026-08-01) | **Resolved** |
| C-243 | 3 | **The audit's history-versus-defect classification compares ISO timestamps lexicographically and never validates the offset.** `_add_timeline` (`modules/appwrite/reconcile.py`) splits orphan files into "predates the first metadata document" (history) and "since" (the anomaly) using `created < report.docs_earliest` on raw strings. That is correct only while every timestamp carries the same UTC offset. The docstring states the assumption; nothing enforces it. A single `+02:00` value would silently move a file across the boundary — and that boundary is the input to a **deletion decision** about production buckets. **Understated on first registration (/code-review max, 2026-08-01):** the defect is not only the unvalidated offset. `_created()` accepts **any non-empty string** as a timestamp, so a malformed or differently-formatted value compares lexicographically against valid ones and silently lands on one side of the history/anomaly boundary with no error. Related: C-251, which records what happens to records carrying no timestamp at all. | A document or file written with a non-`+00:00` offset, a malformed `$createdAt`, or an Appwrite change to timestamp rendering **RESOLVED 2026-08-01 (#342, S2).** Timestamps are parsed, not compared as text. `reconcile/timeline.py::parse_timestamp` returns an offset-aware UTC `datetime` or `None`, and `ReconciliationReport`'s four bound fields now hold `datetime` rather than `str` — so the wrong comparison is **unrepresentable**, not merely discouraged. A `+02:00` value is now ordered chronologically against a `Z` one (pinned by a test where the two orderings disagree: 23:00+02:00 is an hour BEFORE 22:00Z, and the old code called it the anomaly). An unparseable value yields `None`, is excluded from the range, is classified as neither history nor anomaly, and is reported in `indeterminate`. | expert-code-review (2026-08-01, Kleppmann finding) + /code-review max (2026-08-01) | **Resolved** |
| C-244 | 4 | **The audit's verdict conflates hygiene with corruption, and its exit code reports a false error.** `is_clean` is False if there are orphans **or** dangling documents **or** duplicate filenames, and all three render as `VERDICT: PAIRING BROKEN`. A duplicate filename is not a broken pairing. `main()` returns exit code 1 for any finding, which makes `conda run` print `ERROR … failed`, reading as a crash. Both inflate alarm on a tool whose entire value is being believed — and both contributed to a production scare. | The next run that finds duplicates and nothing else, or the next person who reads the exit code as a crash and stops running the audit — or the first caller to consult `is_clean` directly **RESOLVED 2026-08-01 (#342, S2).** Three separate properties now, instead of one overloaded one: `pairing_is_broken` (orphans or dangling — the actual corruption), `has_hygiene_findings` (duplicate names), and `is_clean`, which is `not (pairing_is_broken or indeterminate)`. A bucket nobody could read no longer reports itself clean, and duplicates no longer render as PAIRING BROKEN — the verdict for that case is now *"PAIRING INTACT — but N duplicate file name(s) found (hygiene, not corruption)"*. Exit codes moved to `report.exit_code()`: **0** clean or hygiene-only, **1** broken pairing, **2** could not complete. Hygiene exiting 0 is deliberate — exit 1 makes `conda run` print `ERROR … failed`, and a tool that reads as crashing over a duplicate filename stops being run. One pre-existing test asserted the old conflation (`assert not report.is_clean` for a duplicates-only bucket) and was updated with the reasoning inline. | expert-code-review (2026-08-01, Ousterhout finding) + /code-review max (2026-08-01) | **Resolved** |

| C-247 | 3 | **ADR-005's RED/BEIGE/GREEN taxonomy has no fidelity axis, so a healthy palette can certify a false premise.** The taxonomy classifies tests by *intent* — prove it works, probe boundaries, attack it — and the coverage table mandates category mixes per module class. It says nothing about whether a test can observe the real substrate. On 2026-08-01 the Appwrite seam scored 19% RED, comfortably above every target in the ADR, while 90% of its tests could only fail if the code disagreed with a mock written from the same false belief as the code (C-218's second instance, 436 phantom orphans reported against production). **Balanced colours over a wrong premise are uniformly wrong**, and the ADR provides no vocabulary for saying so. Proposed amendment: a fourth property, orthogonal to colour — whether a test drives real behaviour (real SDK, subprocess, recorded response) or a hand-written double — reported alongside category balance. The repo already has the mechanism in two places (`tests/fixtures/wire_contract/`, `feature_frame_contract/`); it is not applied to this seam. Cross-ref C-21 (the measurement), C-218 (the failure mode), C-246 (the phantom tier). | When ADR-005 is next amended, or when category balance is first used to argue a module is adequately tested **RESOLVED 2026-08-01 (#350, S10).** ADR-005 now carries a **fidelity axis**, orthogonal to RED/BEIGE/GREEN and added by amendment rather than rewrite (the repo's convention — original text retained and still in force, per ADR-055's amendment-table style). Colour states a test's **intent**; fidelity states whether it can **observe anything the author did not already believe**. Four levels: *substrate* (the real dependency, or a subprocess/AST observation of real code), *recorded* (a captured response), *derived* (a double built from the dependency's own source or encoding), *asserted* (a hand-written double). The requirement added: **any module talking to an external substrate must have at least one test at substrate or recorded fidelity for each behaviour a wrong belief would silently corrupt**, and a colour distribution quoted without fidelity is an incomplete claim. The 2026-08-01 episode is cited as its evidence — 19% RED, comfortably above every target in the table, over a suite that could not fail. The amendment is explicit that an asserted test is not a bad test: most tests should be asserted. The failure is having **only** them at a boundary where the substrate can surprise you, and then reading a healthy colour balance as safety. | test-review (2026-08-01) | **Resolved** |

| C-253 | 2 | **A vendor SDK is a hard dependency of the platform's most-depended-upon package, and three repos that never mention it are forced to install it.** `pyproject.toml:23` declares `appwrite = ">=13.4.1,<14.0.0"` in `[tool.poetry.dependencies]` — not an extra — and `views_pipeline_core/modules/appwrite/` + `modules/datastore/` hold **4,609 lines** of vendor-specific code. Measured consequences, not asserted ones: **views-hydranet, views-baseline and views-evaluation contain ZERO references to Appwrite in code or in their own `pyproject.toml`, and all three install the SDK transitively** (CRP); views-models and views-postprocessing DO use it and declare it nowhere, free-riding on the transitive dependency (same class as C-144/C-216). **SDP is inverted**: pipeline-core is the platform's most-depended-upon package — five repos pinned — and it depends on a vendor SDK whose entire `databases.list*` surface deprecated at server 1.8.0 (C-245), i.e. the most stable component depends on one of the least stable things in the system. **SRP**: pipeline-core's reasons to change now include Appwrite response shapes (C-217/C-219), pagination (C-241), deprecations (C-245), credentials (C-11/C-229/C-230/C-240), provisioning (C-228/C-233) and error taxonomy (C-231/C-235) — none about forecasting. **REP**: a one-line paging fix needs a pipeline-core release five pinned repos must adopt, for a bug four cannot hit. **The counter-example is on this platform and it is decisive:** views-models solves the same class of problem in **681 lines of stdlib `urllib` with no SDK dependency, an explicit timeout** (which pipeline-core still lacks — C-15/#248), confined to `tools/liveness/` and imported by nothing in `models/`, `ensembles/` or `postprocessors/`. It does less (read-only liveness, not write+metadata+dedup+cache), so 681 does not replace 4,609 — but it proves the SDK is not required, that vendor contact can be confined to a named directory, and that a timeout is achievable. **Not a D8 matter** (see C-252): D8 asks whether views-appwrite should host a shared client; this asks why the forecasting library knows what Appwrite is at all. Cheapest correct move, and it needs no cross-repo coordination or new package: make `appwrite` an optional extra, lazy-import behind the existing `PredictionSaver` Protocol (`managers/prediction/savers.py:44`). **Blocker found by falsification 2026-08-01, and it makes the remedy larger than one line:** the module that DEFINES the Protocol also does `from appwrite.exception import AppwriteException` at module scope (`savers.py:17`; same at `managers/prediction/io.py:15`), so `import views_pipeline_core.managers.prediction.savers` **loads the vendor SDK today** — verified by subprocess. Making `appwrite` optional would break importing the Protocol itself. The bare package import is already clean, so the blast radius is exactly those two modules. `savers.py` must be split first — the Protocol and the local savers apart from the Appwrite saver — which is also the one-concept-per-file outcome the file-structure rules ask for. Pinned by `tests/test_falsification_epic_339_readiness.py::test_f1*`, and add a subprocess purity guard in the exact idiom of `tests/test_import_purity.py`, which shipped for pandas on 2026-07-30 and works. Physical relocation of the 4,609 lines is a separate, later question. Both used it identically, in one `except (ConnectionError, TimeoutError, OSError, AppwriteException)` clause. Resolved by `managers/prediction/vendor_faults.py::upload_transport_faults()`, which resolves the vendor exception lazily and **omits it when the extra is absent** — an `AppwriteException` cannot be raised by an SDK that is not installed, so the narrower tuple loses nothing. Six more probes in `test_import_purity.py` pin that nothing on the delivery path loads the SDK. **The two F1 stubs converted themselves:** marked `xfail(strict=True)`, they XPASSed the moment the imports were removed and the suite refused to stay green until they were rewritten as plain assertions — the ratchet firing as designed. | C-254 | 2 | **Vendor exit cost is unmeasured and has never been tested.** The platform calls **26 distinct Appwrite operations** from pipeline-core (8 storage, 14 databases, 4 account/session), 22 more from views-faoapi's own copy, and has **never stood up an alternative or timed a recovery**. ADR-047 bounds the blast radius honestly and it is smaller than it feels: local disk is AUTHORITATIVE and views-forecasts is PRIMARY EXTERNAL, so if Appwrite terminated service **the pipeline keeps running and no forecast is lost** — what stops is the **FAO delivery**, an external commitment to a real counterparty, plus views-models' liveness tooling. The exposure is therefore not existential; it is that **nobody knows how long restoration takes**, and an unmeasured recovery time is indistinguishable from an unbounded one. Decomposed: the 4 account/session ops are dead (C-255); the 8 storage ops are the S3 verb set and are commodity, ~1–2 days against MinIO/S3/filesystem; the 14 database ops are the genuine lock-in — **but they store an index over files, and this platform already publishes an index** (ADR-013 manifests, in production for the FAO delivery). Two mechanisms needed to retire them already exist in our code: caller-supplied file ids (`file.py:1719`, `:1807` — currently defaulted to `ID.unique()`) and server-side ordering on storage (`list_files(order_field="$createdAt", order_type="DESC")`, `file.py:2418-2441`). Content-addressed ids would also delete the dedup-query path that produced C-231, C-232 and the 436-phantom-orphan incident — the vendor-independence work and the Cluster J work are the same work. Remedy is a **drill, not a migration**: stand up MinIO in a container, point a copy of the delivery at it, and record the elapsed time; repeat annually. Related: C-253 (the dependency structure), C-241, C-245, Cluster J. | Any Appwrite pricing or terms-of-service change, the next contract renewal, or the 2026-11-30 key expiry — whichever comes first; and if none has occurred, on the anniversary of the last drill | expert-code-review (2026-08-01, vendor exit-cost review) + maintainer escalation | Open |

C-241 fixes exactly **one** of them. Not all of them are defects — listing databases or collections in a project holding one and two respectively is bounded by reality — but **which are bounded has never been decided**, and every count produced so far — "~12", then "16" — was wrong, in a register entry whose whole point is that the population was never measured. **The guard intended to catch this class is itself mis-specified:** a check that looks for `Query.limit` among a call's arguments flags CORRECT code, because `list_files` (`:2460`) and the repaired `reconcile.py` both build a `queries` list, append the limit, and pass the variable. A guard that flags correct code gets allowlisted into uselessness — the failure mode where a check teaches people to ignore it. Remedy: resolve limits bound before the call, and classify each site as bounded-by-reality or genuinely unbounded, in writing. Pinned by `tests/test_falsification_epic_339_readiness.py::test_f2*`. Cluster J. | When the read-completeness guard is written (#343) — it must carry the real count and must not flag a limit bound to a variable | falsify (2026-08-01, epic #339 readiness audit) **RESOLVED 2026-08-01 (#343).** The guard bounded all of them: four unique-key lookups gained `Query.limit(1)`, `_require_containers` gained a stated page plus a refusal to confirm an absence from a partial read, and provisioning's three exists-then-create lookups gained `_complete_listing`. **The line-number worklist above went stale in the same change that resolved it** — #343's own edits shifted every citation, and `reconcile.py:223` ceased to exist when #342 turned that module into a package. Kept as written rather than silently repaired, because a worklist that rots between being written and being executed is the more useful lesson: the guard is now the enumeration, and it re-derives itself on every run. **The measurement question this entry raised is settled the same way** — the guard reports 0 unbounded of 17 `list*` calls in the governed directories, 2 allowlisted as bounded-in-reality and 1 suppressed as a tracked defect, and nobody has to count by hand again. | **Resolved** |
| C-257 | 2 | **A failed delete of the OLD metadata card is swallowed, leaving exactly the dangling document the Appwrite shelf audit (`modules/appwrite/audit/`) exists to enumerate.** `upload_file_with_metadata` (`modules/appwrite/file.py`, the `if old_doc_id:` branch) wraps `databases.delete_document` in `try/except Exception` and responds with `logger.warning(f"Failed to delete old metadata: {e}")`, then continues to a successful upload. The old document survives, still pointing at the file that was just replaced — a **dangling document**, the inverse defect of an orphan file and one of the two shapes `modules/appwrite/audit/` was built to count. **What makes this more than an oversight: the same function is careful about the file and careless about the card.** Twenty lines above, the branch that deletes the OLD FILE returns a failure `OperationResult` whose message says, in as many words, that proceeding *"would orphan it"*. The author had the exact thought one branch earlier and did not carry it across — the C-242 asymmetry again, inside a single function. **Found by the Cluster J guard on its first run (#343)**, which is the guard doing precisely what it was built for: not one of the sites anybody had listed. Not fixed in #343 because the correct behaviour interacts with ADR-047's write-failure policy — the upload itself succeeded and ADR-047 says an Appwrite write failure must not fail the run — so the choice is between returning PARTIAL_SUCCESS (and making every caller handle it, cf. C-227) and scheduling a repair. That is a design decision deserving its own change, not a rider on the story that adds the guard. Suppressed in `tests/test_read_completeness.py::_TRACKED_DEFECTS`, a dictionary kept deliberately separate from the justified-exemption one and gated by a test requiring every entry to name a register ID — an exemption that says "this is broken and tracked" must not be filed next to ones that say "this is fine". Cluster J, shape three. | C-258 | 1 | **The deduplication fallback walks the whole bucket with a short-page terminator and no total-guard, and answers NOT_FOUND — so an incomplete read authorises a duplicate upload.** `_file_exists_by_hash`'s FOUND_BY_NAME fallback (`modules/appwrite/file.py`, the `except AppwriteException` branch) ran `while True`, broke on `len(files_chunk) < limit`, advanced `offset += limit` by what it ASKED for rather than what it received, never compared against the substrate's reported `total`, and had no page backstop — in a file that defines `MAX_METADATA_PAGES` twenty lines away. Appwrite may grant fewer rows than the requested limit, so a capped page ends the walk early; the filename is then absent from `all_files` and the method returns **`NOT_FOUND`**, which its caller reads as *"no duplicate exists"* and uploads one. A partial read producing a false absence, on the deduplication path. Tier 1: wrong action, no error signal. **This is the third occurrence of one pattern, and the pattern is the finding.** C-242 recorded it at *function* scale (one walk certified, its sibling not); #342's review caught it at *story* scale (#341's rule not carried into #342's code); this is *file* scale — #341 fixed `search_files_by_metadata` and nobody asked what else in the same file pages. Found only by the S0–S3 retrospective sweep, which enumerated every paging walk in the repo and checked each against the rule rather than reviewing changesets one at a time. **It also exposes a real limitation of the C-256 guard, which is the more valuable half:** the call carries `Query.limit(limit)`, so `test_read_completeness` is satisfied — **the guard governs whether a limit is SUPPLIED, not whether the walk TERMINATES correctly**. That is now stated in the guard's docstring, and the walk-shape property is covered by tests instead. Fixed by the #341 rule (empty-page terminator, advance by received, page guard, total-guard) plus a refusal to return NOT_FOUND from a walk that did not finish. Cluster J, shapes one and two. Related: C-241, C-242, C-231, C-232. | The next duplicate check against a bucket where the substrate caps a page below the requested limit — or the next paging walk added anywhere in this repo without checking it against the rule | code-review max, S0–S3 retrospective sweep (2026-08-01) | **Resolved** |

The deeper fix — branching on `e.type` rather than on prose — remains C-235. | C-15 | 3 | **No timeouts on external operations (partially mitigated).** Ensemble subprocess execution now has a 7200s timeout (2026-04-04). viewser data fetching, Appwrite uploads, and WandB API calls still run without explicit timeouts. **Appwrite dimension confirmed (2026-07-30, þing-01 → #325):** no timeout or deadline is set on any Appwrite HTTP call in this repo — the SDK inherits `requests`' default of *none*, so a hung call hangs the whole delivery indefinitely rather than failing. Own instance of a twin entry on the views-appwrite register. Note the fix is itself a behavior change: introducing a timeout field converts an indefinite hang into a raised timeout on paths that currently have no error handling for it (C-227) — drill the hang path before shipping a value. | When a timeout value is first introduced on any Appwrite/viewser/WandB call — drill the hang path first, since the change converts an indefinite hang into a raise on paths that today have no handler for it (C-227) **RESOLVED 2026-08-01 (#347, S7).** **The drill ran first, as þing-01 required, and its finding was unambiguous:** a transport that never returns left `list_files`, `search_files_by_metadata` and `get_bucket` all *STILL BLOCKED after 3s (no timeout)* — no timeout, no error, no recovery. An indefinite hang is worse than an error because nothing downstream ever learns anything. **The SDK offers no hook** — `appwrite.client.Client` calls `requests.request(...)` with no `timeout=` and exposes no `set_timeout`, verified against the installed version — so `modules/appwrite/transport.py` injects one at the reference the SDK resolves. That is invasive and deliberately the narrowest invasive option: it does not touch the global `requests` module, does not use `socket.setdefaulttimeout()` (which would silently bound viewser, wandb and everything else in the process), does not copy `Client.call` (which would need re-verifying on every SDK bump), is idempotent, and defers to an explicit `timeout=` if the SDK ever passes one. Default **30s**, overridable via `APPWRITE_REQUEST_TIMEOUT_SECONDS`; a malformed or non-positive override **raises** rather than falling back, because silently defaulting is the Cluster J shape at the configuration boundary. A test fails the day the SDK grows `set_timeout`, with instructions to delete the workaround — a vendor workaround that outlives its reason becomes folklore. | The next Appwrite call made over a stalled connection | expert-code-review + þing-01 | **Resolved** |

Related: C-14 (resolved — pipeline-core side), C-30 (no cross-repo contract test). | When a new data source is added to views-pipeline-core, or the cache filename convention changes, engine repos fail with `FileNotFoundError` because they independently hardcode `_viewser_df` | expert-review (2026-04-27)| **Resolved for this repo (2026-08-10, #416); cross-repo remainder tracked.** **UPDATE 2026-08-10 (#416, epic #410): pipeline-core's half was NOT done, contrary to what #154 and the epic both assumed.** The constants existed — `CACHE_FILENAME_TEMPLATE`, `CACHE_SOURCES`, `FRAME_CACHE_DIRNAME_TEMPLATE`, with `data/constants.py` naming itself the single source of truth and the frame template even carrying a comment citing this concern — and `ModelPathManager._get_raw_data_file_paths` **still built its match prefix by hand** as `f"{run_type}_{src}_df"`. It agreed with the template, which is precisely why it survived: a change to the template would have left it behind silently and the loader would have stopped finding its own caches. **Constants existing is not constants being used, and only a check can tell the difference** — the epic's scoping said this half was complete, and it was wrong. Now derived via `cache_filename_prefix()` and guarded by `tests/test_cache_name_has_one_spelling.py`, which scans the package for the convention's *constructed* shape (a placeholder, a source or placeholder, then the marker) while excluding docstrings — documentation may describe the convention, code may not construct it. Two earlier versions of that guard were broader and flagged dozens of docstrings; a guard that fires on documentation gets switched off (#415 taught this once already). Six mutations verified, including one that must NOT fire. **Cross-repo, verified rather than taken from #154's list:** views-hydranet#256 and views-r2darts2#25 filed against live constructions; **views-models needed no issue** — #154 named `bright_starship`'s Phase 1 workaround; it was removed in `fd65b098` under views-models#80, which is closed. The only remaining reference is a read-only glob in an investigation script, reachable from no training or forecasting entrypoint. #154's list was stale there too — filing against it would have repeated this concern's own error. (Register text first said 'not filed'; review pointed out 'already tracked and closed' is the accurate framing, and the difference matters: one implies an omission.) **A latent coupling was also found and pinned:** since #412 a sidecar sits beside each cache, and its filename *stem* starts with the cache prefix — so `_get_raw_data_file_paths`'s `startswith` matches it. It is excluded only by the separate suffix filter, which predates the sidecar and knows nothing about it. Verified safe by hand during review, then given a test, because a hand-verified invariant with no test is one refactor from being wrong; mutation-confirmed by removing the suffix filter. |

| C-61 | 2 | **No cache provenance verification when `use_saved=True` — stale data loaded silently if index matches.** `get_data()` (`dataloaders.py:1431-1439`) loads cached parquet when `use_saved=True` without verifying the cache was produced by the current config. `CoreDataSniffer` validates MultiIndex structure (level, index names, partition range) but NOT column content, feature set, or descriptor hash. If a model changes its queryset features or region while a cached parquet from the old config exists with the same level, the sniffer passes and training proceeds on stale features. C-14's resolution (source-encoded filenames) handles source-switching staleness; this concern is the explicitly deferred "broader data-versioning concern (queryset hashes, manifests)" from C-14. **Additional dimension (2026-06-03, falsification):** `use_saved=True` with a *missing* cache file silently falls back to a network fetch (`dataloaders.py:1504-1506`). The `saved` flag semantically means "prefer cache, fall back to fetch" — not "require cached data." This means the three-site `saved` invariant (C-146) provides a weaker guarantee than its documentation implies: non-training runs may still trigger fresh data fetches when cache files are absent. Related: C-14 (resolved — source dimension only), C-60 (workaround creates false-provenance cache), C-146 (three-site invariant — relies on `saved` semantics). | When a developer changes a model's queryset features, region, or feature rename mapping, then runs with `--saved` (or a workaround sets `args.saved=True`), stale cache with correct index but wrong features is loaded without error; OR when cache is absent, `use_saved=True` silently fetches fresh data instead of failing | expert-review (2026-04-27) + falsify (2026-06-03) | **Resolved (2026-08-10, epic #410, ADR-059).** A cache is served only if its provenance record matches the current run: queryset digest, source, partition, month range and level. Mismatch refuses and names every differing field; an absent record refetches (every cache predating the epic has none, and refetching gives the identical guarantee); an unreadable one refuses (damaged is not missing); another version refetches. Both cache paths — pandas and FeatureFrame — are covered; a mutation removing verification from the frame path went undetected until a test for it existed, so the epic could have closed with half the surface unguarded. **Bounded, and the bound is stated:** the digest covers the specification, not the data, so an upstream revision under an unchanged queryset is not detected (C-285, open). |

| C-165 | 3 | **`data/` package is in the SAP pain zone — most stable internal package with zero abstractions.** `data/` has the lowest instability ratio in the codebase (I=0.14 — 12 incoming consumers, 2 outgoing deps) yet contains zero protocols, zero ABCs, zero abstract classes. All 3 primary classes are fully concrete: `PredictionFrame` (`prediction_frame.py`, 167 LOC), `_ViewsDataset` (`handlers.py`, 953 LOC), `ModelPathManager` (`model_path.py`, 959 LOC). SAP says: a component this stable should be proportionally abstract so it can survive change without forcing 12 consumers to adapt. `ModelPathProtocol` in `types.py` was extracted specifically to address this for `ModelPathManager` — proving the fix pattern works — but no corresponding protocol exists for `PredictionFrame` or `_ViewsDataset`. `PredictionFrame` is the #1 degree-centrality hub (456 edges, graphify 2026-06-02) and growing fastest, making its absence of a protocol the highest-leverage SAP gap. When PredictionFrame's interface needs to evolve (new required identifier, sparse array support, metadata field), every consumer must change simultaneously. The `data/` package's concrete stability currently works because PredictionFrame's interface is deliberately minimal — but that's stability through rigidity, not stability through abstraction. Related: C-48 (concrete dependencies — consumer-side view of same gap), C-36 (_ViewsDataset god class — the class that makes the package especially painful to change), C-38 (no domain entity layer — the broader architectural context). **Strategic direction (2026-06-19):** the `PredictionFrameProtocol`/`FeatureFrameProtocol` this calls for is planned to live in **views-frames** (scaffolded 2026-06-19), NOT in core's `data/` — the abstraction belongs in a stable leaf package every repo depends on (SDP+SAP), which also de-duplicates datafactory's `FeatureFrame`. | When `PredictionFrame` needs to evolve (e.g., add a required identifier for model provenance, support sparse/chunked y_pred for streaming evaluation, or add metadata fields for the prediction store) — 12 consumers across 5 packages must adapt simultaneously because no `PredictionFrameProtocol` absorbs the interface change. Same when _ViewsDataset Phase 2 decomposition (C-36) begins: every consumer typed against the concrete class must be updated | package principles audit (2026-06-06) | Open |
| C-166 | 3 | **Blanket `except Exception` on Appwrite uploads swallows programming bugs at 2 independent sites.** `AppwriteSaver.save()` (`savers.py:150-153`) and `PredictionIOManager._upload_to_prediction_store()` (`io.py:140-143`) both catch `except Exception` on Appwrite uploads and log without re-raising. Deliberate design: an Appwrite outage should not block the pipeline (predictions are already persisted locally via NpzSaver/LocalParquetSaver). However, the blanket catch also swallows `TypeError`, `AttributeError`, `KeyError` from programming bugs — wrong arguments to `upload_data()`, renamed fields in `PredictionMetadata`, or `DatastoreModule` API changes. These programming errors are silently logged and lost, making integration bugs with Appwrite invisible until manual inspection of logs. The docstring on `AppwriteSaver` explicitly documents "Graceful degradation" — but the "Fail Loud and Proud" principle (D-16 resolution) should apply to programming errors even when network errors are tolerated. Fix: narrow the catch to network/API exceptions (`requests.RequestException`, `AppwriteException`, `ConnectionError`, `TimeoutError`) and let programming errors propagate. **Falsification update (2026-06-06):** `io.py` has 3 `except Exception` blocks (lines ~103, ~140, ~210), not 1 as originally documented. Lines ~103 and ~210 re-raise as `PipelineException` (not silent), but are still overly broad. Sprint targets the silent catch at line ~140 only. Also, `requests` is a transitive dependency (via appwrite SDK), not declared in `pyproject.toml` — catching `requests.RequestException` is fragile. Prefer stdlib alternatives: `OSError`, `ConnectionError`, `TimeoutError`. **Verified narrowed at both named sites (2026-07-30):** `savers.py:153` and `io.py:142` now catch `(ConnectionError, TimeoutError, OSError, AppwriteException)`, not bare `Exception` — the sprint target landed. Remaining scope: `io.py`'s other two broad blocks (~:103, ~:210, both re-raising). **The narrowing did not make these sites fail loud** — the failures that actually occur there arrive as return values, not exceptions (C-227). Related: C-227 (the in-band failure path at the same two sites), C-15 (no timeouts — different concern, same external operation), C-44 (ISP on AppWriteFileModule — different concern, same Appwrite surface), D-16 (Resolved — Fail Loud for configured operations). | A developer changes `DatastoreModule.upload_data()` signature, renames a `PredictionMetadata` field, or introduces a type error in the upload arguments — the `except Exception` silently swallows the resulting `TypeError`/`AttributeError`, predictions appear to save successfully but never reach Appwrite | expert-code-review (2026-06-05, Data Format Landscape review) + falsify (2026-06-06, sprint splash zone P2/P6) | Open |
| C-167 | 3 | **ReconciliationModule crosses the pipeline-core↔views-reporting boundary with no integration test on output schema.** `ensemble.py:747` and `dataframe_ensemble.py:921` import `ReconciliationModule` from views-reporting, pass it `_CDataset` and `_PGDataset`, and save the reconciled output. No test verifies the I/O schema contract at this boundary. If views-reporting changes ReconciliationModule's output format (columns, dtypes, index structure), pipeline-core's reconciled forecasts change silently — no assertion, no schema check, no integration test. The reconciliation path is also architecturally mislocated: it's a core data operation (PGM-CM level reconciliation during forecasting) living in a presentation package. **Planned relocation:** `views-post-processing` is the intended new home — semantically correct as post-inference data processing, not presentation. Relocation should include defining a typed protocol or dataclass for the reconciliation I/O boundary. **Strategic direction (2026-06-19):** that typed I/O boundary = views-frames frame types; relocation should consume those and remove the cross-repo `reconciled_dataframe` mutation (C-182). **#195 UPDATE (PR #217, pending merge to development):** the views-reporting import is **removed** — both managers now reconcile via an injected `Reconciler` port (`domain/reconciliation.py`) + a views-frames-only `reconcile_datasets` adapter (`modules/reconciliation/adapter.py`). The cross-repo *coupling* this entry tracked is gone; the residual is now the **new** boundary's contract: (a) **#196 real-run parity** must confirm the adapter+injected reconciler reproduce the legacy output, and (b) a **behavior delta** — the legacy `reconcile()` caught per-(country,time,target) worker failures, logged+alerted and continued (failed cells kept original values); the new path lets any `reconciler.reconcile()` exception abort the whole reconciliation (intentionally more Fail-Loud, but #196 parity must account for it). Note the adapter realigns reconciled rows by `(time,unit)` and fails loud on missing/duplicate keys (silent-scatter guard added in review). **K-FINAL (2026-06-26, views-frames v1.7.0 + 8-expert review):** the concrete injected through the port is **`views_frames_reconcile.ReconciliationModule`** (the v1.7.0 sibling), **not** views-postprocessing — built + injected by **views-models** at the composition root (geography via its `ViewserCountryMappingProvider`, baked into the injected instance). **Decision K** (keep the port) was confirmed over Decision C: the reconciler is a *stateful, geography-bearing* collaborator (composition belongs at the root), so the port is a legitimate DIP seam; pipeline-core never imports the concrete and never sees geography. Parity is owned by views_frames_reconcile's own bit-identity gate (views-frames#135). Related: C-182 (cross-repo mutation — also addressed by #195), C-18 (ReconciliationModule unit test coverage — different scope), C-125 (Resolved — CRP heavyweight dependency, mitigated by deferred imports), C-194 (adapter df-copy memory), D-28 (ReconciliationModule location), D-16 (Resolved — Fail Loud for configured reconciliation). | #196 real-run parity testing of the new adapter+injected-reconciler boundary, OR a production reconciler that raises on a degenerate country/grid subset (legacy would skip-and-continue; new aborts the whole reconciliation) | expert-code-review (2026-06-06, dependency direction review) + review-diff (2026-06-26, #195 adapter) | Open |
